studies 3 August 2026, 00:01 AM In Focus DENGUE OUTBREAK DENV-3 now dominant strain Say experts; hospitals report rising admissions 4 August 2026, 00:00 AM BANGLADESH SHARE Helemul Alam Helemul Alam Unable to find a seat, a woman sits on the floor with her child at Mugda Medical College Hospital in Dhaka. The child has had a fever for four days, and doctors suspect dengue while awaiting test results. According to the DGHS, at least three people died and 580 others were hospitalised with dengue in the 24 hours until 8:00am yesterday, taking this year’s death toll to 59 and total cases to 16,684. PHOTO: MEHEDI HASAN The DENV-3 serotype has become the dominant strain of the dengue virus in Bangladesh this year, replacing DENV-2, which was the most prevalent last year, according to the Institute of Epidemiology, Disease Control and Research (IEDCR). A recent IEDCR survey of 71 dengue-positive patients found that 65 (91.55 percent) were infected with DENV-3, while the remaining six (9.23 percent) had DENV-2. Tap here to add The Daily Star as a trusted source By comparison, last year’s surveillance found DENV-2 in 49.2 percent of samples, followed by DENV-3 (35.8 percent), DENV-1 (8.8 percent), and DENV-4 (6.3 percent), IEDCR Director Dr Quazi Ahmed Zaki told The Daily Star. According to dengue experts, the virus has four serotypes -- DENV-1, DENV-2, DENV-3 and DENV-4 -- each comprising several genotypes. Although DENV-2 has historically been linked to major outbreaks, disease severity depends on multiple factors rather than the serotype alone. Zaki cautioned against describing any serotype as inherently more dangerous. “It would not be scientifically accurate to label one dengue serotype as more severe on its own. Disease severity depends on factors including the viral strain, the patient’s immune response, and underlying illnesses,” he said. Zaki said the distribution of serotypes also varies across the country. “Samples from Dhaka and other urban healthcare facilities are different from those collected in the hill tracts and other rural areas,” he said. Image “The overall picture is mixed. Urban areas may show a higher concentration of one serotype, but you cannot generalise the findings for the entire country,” he said. The IEDCR director said previous dengue infection remains one of the strongest risk factors for severe disease. “People who have had dengue before face a higher risk of severe complications, including dengue shock syndrome, if they are infected again,” he said. Patients with kidney disease, liver disease, cancer, or other active infections are also at greater risk of severe illness, regardless of the infecting serotype or whether it is their first dengue infection, he added. He urged public awareness campaigns to focus on repeat infections and underlying health conditions rather than portraying one serotype as universally more dangerous. According to the Directorate General of Health Services, 59 people had died from dengue and 16,684 had been hospitalised this year as of August 3. Meanwhile, physicians say dengue admissions have increased sharply over the past two weeks. HM Nazmul Ahsan, associate professor at Shaheed Suhrawardy Medical College Hospital, said the hospital has opened two dedicated dengue wards -- one for male and the other for female patients -- after admissions rose to 15-20 patients in each admission cycle. Referring to the latest survey, he said both DENV-2 and DENV-3 are circulating this season, although DENV-3 appears to be more common. Among the four serotypes, DENV-2 and DENV-3 are more severe, Nazmul said. However, the risk of death is higher if a person who previously had dengue is infected with a different serotype, he added. He said many patients are going into shock rapidly, but clinicians have become more experienced in managing severe dengue. For all latest news, follow The Daily Star's Google News channel. “We are seeing patients who deteriorate quite quickly. However, management has improved, and most patients are receiving timely treatment,” he said. Doctors are also reporting a rise in dengue patients with hepatitis this year. “We are seeing considerably more dengue patients with hepatitis than in previous years. Some patients are also dealing with cardiac complications,” he said. He explained that dengue-associated hepatitis requires more than routine fluid management. “When dengue causes hepatitis and liver enzyme levels become markedly elevated, treatment has to be adjusted. These patients require closer monitoring and additional medications beyond standard dengue management,” he said. Ahsan urged people with persistent fever or warning signs to seek medical care promptly, saying early diagnosis and timely treatment remain the best way to prevent severe complications and deaths. 印度尼西亚登革热 疫苗 原型复兴 mRNA 错误信息 - 雅虎

Indonesia dengue vaccine prototype revives mRNA misinformation - Yahoo
新闻简报印度尼西亚研究人员开发了一种基于 mRNA 的登革热 疫苗 原型,引发了新冠病毒时代错误信息的死灰复燃。社交媒体上流传的虚假说法称,疫苗 会改变 DNA 并由人工智能控制,专家和印度尼西亚卫生部对此予以坚决驳斥。工信部澄清,mRNA 疫苗 仅为 免疫应答 提供临时指令,不包含 AI 成分。印度尼西亚每年记录约 151,000 例登革热病例,是受该疾病影响最严重的国家之一。疫苗 仍处于早期开发阶段,在公开使用之前需要经过严格的测试。尽管科学界对 mRNA 安全性达成了共识,但这一事件凸显了打击 疫苗 错误信息所面临的持续挑战。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Indonesia dengue vaccine prototype revives mRNA misinformation - Yahoo

News Brief
Indonesian researchers have developed an mRNA-based dengue vaccine prototype, sparking the resurgence of COVID-era misinformation. False claims circulating on social media allege the vaccine alters DNA and is controlled by AI, which experts and the Indonesian Health Ministry have firmly refuted. The ministry clarified that mRNA vaccines only provide temporary instructions for immune responses and do not contain AI components. Indonesia, which records about 151,000 dengue cases annually, is among the countries most affected by the disease. The vaccine is still in early development stages and requires rigorous testing before public use. This incident highlights ongoing challenges in combating vaccine misinformation despite scientific consensus on mRNA safety.
Original source text (English)
Experts say messenger RNA (mRNA) vaccines do not alter DNA, contrary to social media posts that resurfaced the long-debunked Covid-era conspiracy theory after Indonesian researchers developed a dengue shot prototype. The new jab is also not controlled by artificial intelligence, as the online rumours allege; the Indonesian Health Ministry told AFP that while AI will be used to predict future virus mutations, it is not a component of the vaccine itself. "Indonesia launches dengue vaccine prototype based on mRNA technology which will be controlled by AI and will alter DNA given from God, breaking the spirituality signal to God," reads part of an Indonesian-language Instagram post shared on July 14, 2026. "The purpose of the vaccine is population shrinking". The post, which has been viewed more than 16,000 times, shares footage of anti-vaccine comments from former health minister Siti Fadilah Supari and local politician Dharma Pongrekun, whom AFP has previously fact-checked for spreading misinformation about hantavirus . "Health has become a very convenient tool to accuse and control nations around the world," Supari says in one of the clips. "For example, we have all experienced what is called Covid. How could people all over the world be deceived about Covid? How could the entire world be vaccinated without knowing what was being put into their bodies?" The post caption goes on to echo another conspiracy theory, previously debunked by AFP , about Indonesia's plan to release mosquitoes infected with the Wolbachia bacteria to help prevent the spread of viruses such as dengue. Screenshot of the false post captured on July 28, 2026, with a red X added by AFP Similar claims have also circulated on Facebook . The University of Indonesia, along with Tsinghua University, successfully developed an mRNA-based dengue vaccine prototype on July 8, saying it has the potential to become the first such jab to reach preclinical trials ( archived link ). Data from the Health Ministry show Indonesia records around 151,000 dengue cases every year -- making it among the country's top diseases with high burden or impacts, alongside tuberculosis, HIV and malaria ( archived link ). Dengue, which spreads through bites from infected mosquitoes and causes severe flu-like symptoms, has exploded globally , fuelled by rising temperatures and densely populated cities ( archived link ). According to the World Health Organization (WHO), nearly half the world's population is at risk, with an estimated 100 million to 400 million infections each year. Factfile on the mosquito-borne dengue virus AFP While dengue vaccines already exist, mRNA shots allow production to be scaled up more quickly since a virus need not be grown in a lab -- and they are generally safer than traditional jabs because they do not contain live pathogens ( archived link ). However, mRNA has become a regular target of conspiracy theories since the Covid-19 pandemic, fuelling baseless claims that such shots cause cancer or alter recipients' DNA . The latest from Indonesia are similarly false. "mRNA vaccines do not alter human DNA and are not controlled by AI," a Health Ministry spokesperson told AFP on July 27. "The mRNA components only carry temporary instructions to form an immune response and then naturally break down". How mRNA works Two live-attenuated vaccines are approved for dengue in Indonesia: Qdenga and Dengvaxia ( archived link ). The former is recommended for patients aged 6-16 in high transmission areas who have not been previously infected with the virus, according to the WHO . The latter jab is being phased out of production (archived links here and here ). Unlike those vacci nes, which contain weakened viruses to train the body's immune response, mRNA shots contain genetic materials that instruct human cells to create proteins matching those of the target pathogen ( archived link ). Pfizer-BioNTech and Moderna used the technology in their Covid-19 vaccines developed during the pandemic. How the Pfizer-BioNTech vaccine uses genetic information from SARS-CoV-2 to stimulate the body's immune response John SAEKILaurence CHUAFP Thomas Preiss , a professor at Australia's John Curtin School of Medical Research, said that, contrary to the claims swirling online, mRNA vaccines do not "last very long in the body" ( archived link ). "When injected into a person, the mRNA gets into cells mainly around the injection site, and these cells make a tiny amount of a protein that looks like a part of the pathogen you want to achieve protection against," he told AFP in a July 28 email. "The body's immune system detects this and produces a lasting immune response, just like with other vaccines. So while the mRNA vaccine is long gone, the immune system's cells will 'remember' how to respond should a real pathogen enter the body". Manufacturers are developing mRNA vaccines to protect against other diseases, including influenza and HIV ( archived link ). 'No plausible rationale' Preiss said there is "n o plausible rationale nor credible scientific evidence" that Indonesia's mRNA vaccine prototype can "alter the DNA of cells" -- a view shared by other experts contacted by AFP. Lukas Gerstweiler , a senior lecturer at the School of Chemical Engineering at Adelaide University, said the mRNA does not contain the specialised enzymes required to copy RNA into DNA and insert it into a chromosome ( archived link ). "Therefore, an mRNA vaccine cannot alter a person's DNA in the manner claimed in the social media post," he told AFP on July 27. A researcher dilutes messenger RNA (mRNA) at the Inserm ART-RNAm laboratory of the CHR (Regional Hospital Centre) in Orleans, central France on November 18, 2025 JEAN-FRANCOIS MONIERAFP More Mitch Ganley , a research officer at the Department of Microbiology and Immunology at the University of Melbourne, added that mRNA is a common molecule that is "found in almost every cell in the body and is essential for the body to function" ( archived link ). "mRNA vaccines are no more likely to alter the DNA of the individual receiving the vaccine than their own RNA that is much more ubiquitous and abundant," he said on July 27. The Indonesian Health Ministry told AFP that the vaccine is still in the development stage, and "it must undergo a series of preclinical research stages, clinical trials and very strict evaluations" supervised by public health authorities before it is made available to the public. AFP has for years debunked claims that mRNA vaccines alter one's DNA. AI modelling for mutations Gerstweiler of Adelaide University also said AI cannot control an mRNA vaccine, as it consists only of biological molecules -- normally m RNA, lipids and other ingredients ( archived link ). "It does not contain a computer processor, software, communications equipment, sensors or an energy source. It cannot receive commands, monitor a person or make decisions inside the body," he said. "Describing a vaccine as being 'controlled by AI' confuses the use of AI during its design with the physical properties of the finished vaccine". The Indonesian Health Ministry confirmed AI is not a component of the dengue vaccine prototype. "The use of AI in dengue vaccines is to predict possible changes or potential mutations of the virus in the future," a spokesperson said. "The hope is that, through AI modelling, this dengue vaccine will not only be effective against the strains currently circulating, but will also have the potential to provide protection against dengue strains that may emerge in the future"

Event
Indonesian researchers, in collaboration with Tsinghua University, developed an mRNA-based dengue vaccine prototype on July 8, 2026. This has led to the resurgence of misinformation on social media, falsely claiming the vaccine alters DNA and is controlled by AI.
Scale, impact and risk

Virginia resident has state’s 1st locally acquired case of dengue fever - WTOP

Virginia resident has state’s 1st locally acquired case of dengue fever - WTOP
新闻简报弗吉尼亚州卫生官员确认了该州首例已知的本地感染 登革热 病例,这是一种通常与国际旅行有关的蚊媒病毒。受影响的居民位于弗吉尼亚州北部,近期没有出过该州旅行,因此患病。卫生官员认为,传播 的发生是因为当地蚊子叮咬了一名受感染的旅行者,随后将病毒传播给了第二名居民。虽然公众面临的风险仍然很低,但官员提醒居民在夏季保护自己免受蚊虫叮咬。该案例凸显了防蚊措施的重要性,例如使用美国环保署注册的驱虫剂和消除蚊子滋生的积水。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 8 月 5 日。该事件发生在夏季,弗吉尼亚州的蚊子季节通常从 5 月初到 11 月初。
地点与对象
弗吉尼亚州,特别是北弗吉尼亚州。受影响的人群是弗吉尼亚州居民,最近没有出过该州。
事件
弗吉尼亚州卫生部确认了该州首例已知的本地感染 登革热 病例。该患者最近没有进行过国际旅行,因此感染了蚊媒病毒,很可能是通过与另一名在海外感染的弗吉尼亚州居民接触而接触到的。
规模、影响与风险
本地感染 登革热 确诊病例 1 例。卫生官员表示,公众面临的风险仍然很低。该案例被描述为提醒人们在夏季预防蚊虫叮咬。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员正在调查 传播,他们认为,当地蚊子叮咬了一名受感染的旅行者,然后将病毒传播给了第二名居民。预防措施包括使用 EPA 注册的驱虫剂和排空积水。该案已确认,尚未调查。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Virginia resident has state’s 1st locally acquired case of dengue fever - WTOP

News Brief
Virginia health officials have confirmed the state's first known case of locally acquired dengue fever, a mosquito-borne virus typically associated with international travel. The affected resident, located in Northern Virginia, became ill without recent travel outside the state. Health officials believe transmission occurred when a local mosquito bit an infected traveler and subsequently spread the virus to the second resident. While the risk to the public remains low, officials are reminding residents to protect themselves from mosquito bites during summer months. The case highlights the importance of mosquito prevention measures, such as using EPA-registered repellent and eliminating standing water where mosquitoes can breed.
Original source text (English)

Virginia resident has state’s 1st locally acquired case of dengue fever Catherine Chen | catherine.chen@wtop.com August 5, 2026, 5:43 AM Share This: share on facebook share on X share on threads share on linkedin share on email print The Virginia Department of Health said the patient was sickened by the mosquito-borne virus but had not recently traveled outside the state. Virginia health officials have confirmed the state’s first known case of locally acquired dengue, a viral disease usually associated with international travel. The Virginia Department of Health said the person, who lives in Northern Virginia, became sick but had not recently traveled outside the state. Instead, the patient had been near another Virginia resident who was infected with dengue overseas. Health officials believe a local mosquito likely bit the traveler and later spread the virus to the second resident. Other health news DC region must prepare for disease-carrying mosquitos entering ‘prime-time season’ School backpack overload: Heavy bags can strain kids’ bodies Michigan reports the first 2 US deaths related to a diarrhea-causing parasite Officials said the risk to the public remains low, but the case is a reminder to protect yourself from mosquito bites during the summer months. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, dengue is spread by Aedes mosquitoes and is not passed directly from person to person. Symptoms can include fever, pain behind the eyes, muscle and joint pain, nausea and rash. Most people recover within a week, though some infections can become severe and require hospitalization. Health officials recommend using EPA-registered insect repellent and emptying standing water around homes where mosquitoes can breed. According to the Virginia Department of Health, the state’s mosquito season typically runs from early May through early November. VDH 正在调查弗吉尼亚州北部的 登革病毒,这是有史以来第一例记录在案的本地感染病毒病例

VDH investigating dengue virus in Northern Virginia, first-ever documented case of virus being acquired locally
新闻简报弗吉尼亚州卫生部正在调查弗吉尼亚州北部首例本地感染的 登革病毒 病例。受影响的居民在患病之前没有已知的州外旅行史。卫生官员认为,传播 的发生是因为当地的蚊子叮咬了一名在国际范围内感染该病毒的感染者,然后将其传播给该居民。VDH 与当地卫生部门合作,正在监测局势,并强调在弗吉尼亚州感染该病毒的风险仍然很低。敦促医疗保健提供者考虑发烧并出现眼痛、肌肉痛和关节痛等症状的患者,即使没有旅行史,并向当地卫生部门报告疑似病例。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布日期:2026 年 8 月 4 日。事件日期未指定,但调查正在进行中。
地点与对象
弗吉尼亚州北部,特别是费尔法克斯县和劳登县。受影响人群:发病前不知道曾到过弗吉尼亚州以外地区旅行的居民。
事件
弗吉尼亚州卫生部 (VDH) 正在调查一例 登革病毒 确诊病例。这是弗吉尼亚州首例有记录的本地感染登革热病例。
规模、影响与风险
VDH认为,在联邦内感染该病毒的风险仍然较低。大约每20人中就有1人会出现重症登革热,这是一种医疗紧急情况。
应对、状态与不确定性
VDH 与费尔法克斯县和劳登县卫生部门正在调查此案。卫生官员正在监测更多病例,并建议预防蚊虫叮咬的策略。建议医疗保健提供者在患有急性发热性疾病的患者中考虑登革热,特别是那些没有旅行史但有提示性症状的患者。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

VDH investigating dengue virus in Northern Virginia, first-ever documented case of virus being acquired locally

News Brief
The Virginia Department of Health is investigating the first-ever documented case of locally acquired dengue virus in Northern Virginia. The affected resident had no known travel history outside the state before falling ill. Health officials believe transmission occurred when a local mosquito bit an infected individual who had contracted the virus internationally and then transmitted it to the resident. The VDH, in collaboration with local health departments, is monitoring the situation and emphasizes that the risk of acquiring the virus in Virginia remains low. Healthcare providers are urged to consider dengue in patients with fever and symptoms like eye pain, muscle pain, and joint pain, even without travel history, and to report suspected cases to local health departments.
Original source text (English)

Spanberger announces data centers required to pay for new transmission infrastructure Close Local News VDH investigating dengue virus in Northern Virginia, first-ever documented case of virus being acquired locally 10 News Digital Team Published: August 4, 2026 at 2:47 PM Tags: Virginia, Health, Northern Virginia, Virus, Infection, Dengue, Dengue Virus An Aedes aegypti mosquito is pictured at a laboratory of the Center for Parasitological and Vector Studies (CEPAVE) of the national scientific research institute CONICET, in La Plata, Buenos Aires Province, Argentina, on March 26, 2024. Researchers at CONICET are studying the biology, genetic characteristics and behaviour of the Aedes aegypti mosquitoes, transmitter of dengue, zika and chikungunya, and creating biological control strategies as Argentina is facing a significant growing number of dengue cases. (Photo by Luis ROBAYO / AFP) (Photo by LUIS ROBAYO/AFP via Getty Images) (LUIS ROBAYO, AFP or licensors) The Virginia Department of Health announced they are investigating a confirmed case of dengue virus that occurred in a resident in Northern Virginia. The VDH stated that they, along with the Fairfax County and Loudoun County Health Departments, are investigating a case of dengue virus reported in a resident who did not have known travel outside of Virginia before the illness’s onset. This is the first-ever documented case of locally acquired dengue in the commonwealth. Recommended Videos Authorities said the patient was “in close proximity to a recent internationally acquired case in a Virginian who was diagnosed” recently. The dengue virus is spread to people by infected Aedes species mosquitoes. Health officials believe the transmission occurred when a local mosquito bit the resident who was infected with the internationally acquired case of the virus, which then went on to bite the local resident, resulting in the infection. The VDH believes the potential risk for acquiring the virus in the commonwealth remains low, and they are working to monitor for any additional cases. While some cases of dengue can be asymptomatic or have mild symptoms, around one in 20 people develop severe dengue, which is a medical emergency. The following are symptoms of severe dengue: Severe abdominal pain or tenderness Persistent vomiting (≥3 episodes in 1 hr. or ≥4 episodes in 6 hrs.) Clinical fluid accumulation (e.g., pleural effusion, ascites, or pericardial effusion) Mucosal bleeding Altered mental status Liver enlargement Progressive increase of the hematocrit (in at least 2 consecutive measurements taken 6 hours apart). If you are a healthcare provider, health officials recommend the following: Advise patients on strategies to prevent mosquito bites. This will not only reduce risk of dengue but also of mosquito-borne disease endemic to Virginia, such as West Nile Virus. Aedes mosquitoes, which can transmit dengue virus, breed in water-filled containers that may be as small as a bottle cap. Advise patients to “tip and toss” containers around their homes and businesses that may hold water. Obtain a travel history for all patients presenting with acute febrile illness. Consider dengue in patients presenting with fever who live in or recently traveled to areas with risk of dengue. Consider dengue in patients without travel history if presenting with an acute febrile illness and strongly suggestive symptoms, including eye pain, muscle pain, joint pain, and bone pain. Alert your local health department immediately if you suspect dengue illness in a patient who has no history of travel to areas with risk of dengue or other risk factors for dengue infection, such as receiving a blood or organ transplant. Obtain diagnostic testing in suspect cases of dengue virus infection. Review the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) guidance on dengue case management, including recommendations by patient groups. You can read more information from the VDH here. Northern Virginia, specifically Fairfax County and Loudoun County. Affected population: a resident who did not have known travel outside of Virginia before illness onset.
Event
The Virginia Department of Health (VDH) is investigating a confirmed case of dengue virus. This is the first-ever documented case of locally acquired dengue in the commonwealth of Virginia.
Scale, impact and risk
The VDH believes the potential risk for acquiring the virus in the commonwealth remains low. Around one in 20 people develop severe dengue, which is a medical emergency.
Response, status and uncertainty

Travellers warned as dengue fever outbreak grips popular holiday spot - Nine.com.au
新闻简报前往萨摩亚的旅客请注意,登革热 暴发 已影响到这个热门度假胜地。据 Smartraveller 和 Nine.com.au 报道,自 2026 年 1 月 1 日以来,这个波利尼西亚岛国已记录了 3,500 多例 登革热 确诊病例,其中 150 多人住院治疗,3 人死亡。这种蚊媒疾病促使卫生当局敦促游客采取标准预防措施,包括使用驱虫剂。症状范围从轻微发烧和关节疼痛到需要住院治疗的严重病例。虽然大多数人会在一周内康复,但 重症登革热 可能是致命的。暴发 强调了对前往热带地区的旅行者采取保护措施的重要性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于2026年8月4日。活动时间:2026年1月1日至6月11日。
地点与对象

事件

规模、影响与风险
萨摩亚已有 3,500 多例确诊病例,150 多人住院治疗,3 人死亡。风险级别:未明确说明。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Travellers warned as dengue fever outbreak grips popular holiday spot - Nine.com.au

News Brief
Travellers to Samoa are being warned about a dengue fever outbreak that has affected the popular holiday destination. According to Smartraveller and Nine.com.au, the Polynesian island nation has recorded over 3,500 confirmed cases of dengue fever since January 1, 2026, with more than 150 hospitalisations and three deaths. The mosquito-borne illness has prompted health authorities to urge visitors to take standard precautions, including using insect repellent. Symptoms range from mild fever and joint pain to severe cases requiring hospital care. While most recover within a week, severe dengue can be fatal. The outbreak highlights the importance of protective measures for travellers to tropical regions.
Original source text (English)

The tropics-associated illness is carried by certain species of mosquito. iStock Advertisement Safe Travel New Zealand found that from January 1 to June 11, the nation had more than 3500 confirmed Dengue cases with more than 150 hospitalisations and three deaths. The tropics-associated illness is carried by certain species of mosquito. Dengue fever is mostly imported into Australia by travellers – if a mosquito bites an infected person, that mosquito can become a carrier. Outbreaks in Australia are largely confined to North Queensland. The severity of the illness can range from mild to severe. Most people with dengue fever recover in about a week, according to Health Direct, but other cases can require hospital care. Advertisement Samoa reportedly had more than 3500 confirmed Dengue cases with more than 150 hospitalisations and three deaths. Supplied Occasionally, dengue fever has proven fatal. Severe dengue fever symptoms may develop several days after the initial symptoms and include frequent vomiting, vomiting with blood in it, and unexpected bleeding. People who have any dengue fever symptoms and are concerned for their health should contact their doctor. Advertisement Advertisement Anybody feeling extremely unwell should contact Triple Zero in Australia or emergency health services overseas. Most cases of the virus have been reported in a few locations including New Caledonia, the Cook Islands and Samoa. New Caledonia remains the most affected location with the highest number of reported cases in 2026. Locally-acquired dengue fever case confirmed in Palm Beach County - WPTV

News Brief
Health officials in Palm Beach County, Florida, have confirmed a locally-acquired case of dengue fever, prompting a mosquito-borne illness advisory. The case, reported on August 3, 2026, marks a concerning development as dengue is typically associated with travel to endemic regions. Symptoms include high fever, severe muscle and joint pain, headaches, and sometimes skin rash, typically appearing within 14 days of a mosquito bite. While rarely fatal, the illness requires supportive medical care. Health officials are urging residents to eliminate standing water around homes, use mosquito repellents, and take other preventive measures. Palm Beach County Mosquito Control is coordinating with health officials on surveillance and prevention efforts, and the state continues monitoring for other mosquito-borne illnesses.

萨摩亚登革热:承保范围问题经纪人不能留出索赔时间

Samoa dengue: the coverage question brokers cannot leave to claims time
新闻简报萨摩亚卫生部于 2025 年 4 月宣布出现 登革热 暴发 确诊病例,到 2026 年中期,整个太平洋地区报告了 3,500 多例病例。尽管 暴发 在 Smartraveller 上被列为 1 级,但由于大多数旅行保单中的流行病和已知事件除外,暴发 给保险承保带来了巨大的挑战。由于萨摩亚的医疗设施有限且对外国人来说费用昂贵,经纪人有义务就这些排除情况向客户提供建议,并确保医疗后送覆盖范围。暴发 凸显了旅行者之间的认知差距,许多人没有意识到即使是 1 级建议也会影响保险范围。保险公司对 暴发 的分类各不相同,需要经纪人直接确认。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Samoa dengue: the coverage question brokers cannot leave to claims time

News Brief
Samoa is experiencing a confirmed dengue fever outbreak declared by its Ministry of Health in April 2025, with over 3,500 cases reported across the Pacific region by mid-2026. Despite being classified as Level 1 on Smartraveller, the outbreak creates significant insurance coverage challenges due to epidemic and known-event exclusions in most travel policies. Brokers face obligations to advise clients on these exclusions and ensure medical evacuation coverage, as healthcare facilities in Samoa are limited and costly for foreigners. The outbreak highlights awareness gaps among travelers, with many unaware that even Level 1 advisories can affect insurance coverage. Insurers vary in their classification of the outbreak, necessitating direct confirmation by brokers.
Original source text (English)
CONTINUE TO SITE CONTINUE TO SITE My account Logout Login News Insurance News Industry News Columns Diversity & Inclusion Mergers & Acquisitions Reinsurance TV Transformation Risk Management Resources Annual Guides Business Strategy E-mag Editorial Panels Events Guides IB Talk Insurance Companies Opinion Premium Content Webinars White papers Best In Insurance Specialty Benefits Catastrophe & Flood Claims Construction & Engineering Cyber Environmental Hospitality Legal Insights Life & Health Marine Motor & Fleet Non-Profits & Charities Professionals Risks Property SME Transformation Travel Workers Compensation IBmarkets Underwriters and Insurers Specialisation Contact Us Contact Us E-newsletter Authors Regular Contributors Advertise IBmarkets - Contact Us Australia United States Canada United Kingdom Australia New Zealand Asia Login Dashboard Travel Samoa dengue: the coverage question brokers cannot leave to claims time Samoa dengue: the coverage question brokers cannot leave to claims time An active Ministry of Health outbreak declaration and an insurer epidemic exclusion are not the same problem – and both apply Travel By Roxanne Libatique Aug 05, 2026 Share A dengue fever outbreak in Samoa – formally declared by the Samoan Ministry of Health in April 2025 and acknowledged in a health sub-advisory by the Australian government – creates two separate coverage problems for travel insurance policyholders. Brokers placing Pacific travel need to understand both before a client travels, because neither issue resolves itself at claims time. Two exclusions, not one Samoa retains a Level 1 “Exercise normal safety precautions” designation on Smartraveller. That matters because it means the destination advisory exclusion – which applies when a “Reconsider your need to travel” or “Do not travel” alert is in place – does not automatically void a policy. What it does not mean is that a dengue claim is straightforwardly covered. The first exposure comes from epidemic and pandemic exclusions. Allianz Australia’s current travel insurance product terms, published on its website, state that its Comprehensive Travel Insurance “has a general exclusion, with limited exceptions, against epidemics and pandemics. That means we don’t cover claims that arise from, or are related to, an epidemic or pandemic.” Samoa’s Ministry of Health has declared a dengue outbreak. Whether an individual insurer classifies that outbreak as an “epidemic” for the purposes of this exclusion is a question brokers must put directly to each insurer – the answer will vary by PDS. The second exposure is the known-event exclusion. Allianz Australia’s product terms state that “there is no cover under any benefit of this policy if your claim arises because you didn’t follow advice or a warning that has been issued by the Australian government or a reliable mass media source.” Smartraveller’s advisory for Samoa, last updated August 5, 2026, explicitly states that Samoa’s Ministry of Health has declared a dengue outbreak and that healthcare systems are facing pressures with the increasing number of cases. That advisory has been live, in updated form, since at least April 2025. The Insurance Council of Australia (ICA) confirms the industry position: most travel insurance policies have exclusions for outbreaks of infectious diseases, pandemics, epidemics, and/or known events. Read next: Known-event exclusions activate across expanded Middle East warning zone Scale of the outbreak From January 1 through June 11, 2026, more than 3,500 confirmed dengue cases, 156 hospitalisations, and three deaths were reported across the Pacific region. Six Pacific island countries and territories – American Samoa, Kiribati, New Caledonia, Samoa, Tonga, and Tuvalu – were reporting active outbreaks as of June 11, with New Caledonia recording the highest case numbers in 2026. Why evacuation cover matters more here than elsewhere For any claim that does respond, the financial exposure is high. Smartraveller is explicit: hospital and medical facilities in Samoa are very limited, all foreigners must pay for health services – with cash typically required before treatment – and in the event of a medical emergency, evacuation by air to Australia or New Zealand is likely the only option. There is no helicopter service, blood supplies are often limited, and medical evacuations may be delayed if commercial airlines are used. Smartraveller states that Australians should hold comprehensive travel insurance covering medical evacuations, noting the Australian government will not pay for these costs. Broker obligations are specific Under Australian law, a general insurance broker who is a licensee or authorised representative must provide financial services efficiently, honestly, and fairly. The National Insurance Brokers Association (NIBA) Insurance Brokers Code of Practice requires brokers to act honestly and with integrity and to communicate with clients in a clear and timely manner. Where the insurance market offers different terms that might meet a client’s needs, the broker should take care to explain the range of available cover and the advantages and disadvantages of each, so the client can make an informed choice. Where an active outbreak has been publicly declared at a destination, a broker who places a policy without advising the client that dengue fever may fall within an epidemic exclusion, or within the policy’s known-event provisions, is not meeting that standard. ICA CEO Andrew Hall made the disclosure expectation clear in October 2025: “Understanding your policy and assessing what kinds of activities you plan on undertaking while you’re travelling is the best way to make sure you’re protected. Check Product Disclosure Statements and speak directly to insurers with any questions.” The awareness gap The 2025 ICA and DFAT Travel Insurance Survey, a national survey of 1,001 Australian travellers, found that while around 70% of respondents were aware of Australian government travel advisories, almost half were unaware that travel insurance would likely not cover travel to destinations subject to a Level 4 (“Do not travel”) advisory. Awareness was lower among younger travellers, who were more likely to underestimate the impact of travel advisories on insurance cover. Samoa’s Level 1 status is precisely the condition that creates false comfort. One in seven Australian travellers went overseas without any cover at all, with one in three of those who skipped insurance deciding against it because they considered their destination safe. Read next: Bali theft fears mask travel insurance’s biggest claims risk Exposure scale Australians comprised 24% of all visitors to Samoa in the first half of 2025, making Australia the second-largest source market after New Zealand. For brokers with clients travelling to Samoa now, three actions are non-negotiable: confirm with each insurer how it classifies the Samoa dengue outbreak under its epidemic and known-event exclusions; verify that medical evacuation cover is explicitly included; and document the advice provided to the client. The Australian Financial Complaints Authority (AFCA) complaints environment – where general insurance complaints rose 17% to 34,231 in 2024-25, with denial of claim based on exclusion among the leading complaint categories – makes that documentation commercially as well as professionally important. Fairfax County and other Virginia health departments are investigating the state's first documented locally acquired dengue virus infection, announced on August 5, 2026. Health officials are working to determine the extent of potential exposure and assess any ongoing risk. The investigation involves multiple agencies to ensure comprehensive monitoring and response.

Walk into that same gym a few months later, and it’s noticeably quieter. It isn’t because people stopped wanting to be healthier. It’s because most people eventually run into the same problem: They simply don’t know what to do. You download a workout plan. You watch videos online. You try different machines. You spend time figuring out exercises, wondering if you’re doing them correctly, and hoping you’ll eventually see results. For busy adults, that approach rarely lasts. That’s one reason personalized training has become one of the fastest-growing segments of the fitness industry. Instead of paying for access to equipment, people are investing in coaching, accountability, and a plan that’s built specifically for them. That shift is happening nationwide — and locally, Alloy Personal Training has become the go-to answer for busy adults across Northern Virginia. At Alloy Personal Training, every member follows a personalized program based on their goals, current fitness level, and any limitations they may have. Every workout is coached by a professional trainer, so there’s never any guesswork about what to do next. Most members train just two or three times each week in 55-minute sessions, making it realistic to fit fitness into a busy schedule. Unlike large group classes where everyone performs the same workout, Alloy’s small-group format allows each person to follow their own individualized program while still benefiting from the energy and accountability of training alongside others. For many members, that’s the difference. It’s not about finding more motivation. It’s about removing the obstacles that cause people to quit. You don’t need to spend hours researching workouts. You don’t need to wonder whether you’re making progress. You simply show up, and your coach takes care of the rest. Whether your goal is to build strength, lose weight, improve energy, stay active as you get older, or simply feel better every day, consistency matters more than perfection. And consistency becomes much easier when you have a plan designed specifically for you. That’s why more adults throughout Northern Virginia are moving away from traditional gyms and choosing a more personalized approach to fitness. B routine priorityPublic-health news

1 confirmed case of dengue fever in Miami-Dade, FDOH says - NBC 6 South Florida

News Brief
The Florida Department of Health in Miami-Dade has confirmed one case of dengue fever and issued a mosquito-borne illness alert. Dengue, transmitted by Aedes aegypti mosquitoes, presents with flu-like symptoms such as fever, joint and muscle pain, and sometimes a rash, typically appearing within 14 days of a mosquito bite. The illness is not contagious. Authorities recommend preventive measures, including using bug repellent, treating clothing with permethrin, eliminating standing water, and using air conditioning with screens. No further details on the affected individual or additional cases were disclosed.
Original source text (English)

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dengue fever case confirmed in Palm Beach County, mosquito-borne advisory issued - WPEC

News Brief
Palm Beach County health officials have confirmed a locally acquired case of dengue fever, prompting a mosquito-borne illness advisory. The infected person was bitten by a mosquito in the area, not during travel. While no patient details or specific location were released, authorities are collaborating with Mosquito Control to conduct surveillance and prevention efforts. Residents are advised to use insect repellent, wear protective clothing, drain standing water, and report dead birds to aid disease tracking. The health department continues monitoring other mosquito-borne illnesses like West Nile virus and chikungunya. Anyone experiencing symptoms should seek medical attention.

(Adobe Stock) 0Comment Share topics: Dengue Palm Beach County Mosquito-borne Mosquito Control Health Department Local transmission Insect repellent Standing water PALM BEACH COUNTY, Fla. (CBS12) — Health officials issued a mosquito-borne illness advisory Monday after confirming a locally acquired case of dengue fever in Palm Beach County. The Florida Department of Health in Palm Beach County said it is working with Palm Beach County Mosquito Control to conduct surveillance and prevention efforts following the confirmed infection. A locally acquired case means the person was infected by a mosquito in the area rather than while traveling to a place where dengue is more common. Health officials did not release information about the patient or identify where in Palm Beach County the infection is believed to have occurred. Dengue is spread to people through the bite of an infected mosquito. The illness can cause fever, headache, pain behind the eyes, muscle and joint pain, nausea, vomiting and a rash. Some infections can develop into severe dengue, which may cause serious bleeding, shock or other potentially life-threatening complications. The health department urged residents to protect themselves from mosquito bites by using insect repellent, wearing long sleeves and pants when possible and making sure doors and windows have intact screens. Palm Beach County health officials issued a mosquito-borne illness advisory after confirming a locally acquired case of dengue fever. (WPEC) Residents should also drain standing water from containers around their homes, including buckets, flowerpots, garbage cans, toys, pool covers and birdbaths. Mosquitoes can breed in small amounts of water. See also: Florida leprosy cases reached a record. See where cases are being reported The department said it continues to monitor mosquito-borne illnesses statewide, including West Nile virus, Eastern equine encephalitis, St. Louis encephalitis, malaria, chikungunya and dengue. Residents are also encouraged to report dead birds to the Florida Fish and Wildlife Conservation Commission because bird deaths can help officials track certain mosquito-borne diseases. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dengue case in Miami-Dade triggers a mosquito alert, health officials say

News Brief
Health officials in Miami-Dade County have issued a mosquito alert following a confirmed locally acquired case of dengue. This brings the total number of cases in the county to 21 so far this year, according to the CDC. The Florida Department of Health is urging residents to take precautions to avoid mosquito bites, as dengue is spread through infected Aedes aegypti mosquitoes. Symptoms include fever, severe muscle and joint pain, and sometimes a rash, with no respiratory symptoms typically present. While most people recover within a week, severe cases can lead to serious complications including internal bleeding and organ failure. The health department and mosquito control are coordinating surveillance and prevention efforts, and residents experiencing symptoms are advised to call for testing and guidance.
Original source text (English)
People across Miami-Dade County should take precautions to avoid mosquito bites following a confirmed locally acquired case of dengue, according to the Florida Department of Health. Symptoms of dengue appear within 14 days of a bite from an infected Aedes aegypti mosquito and can present "as a flu-like illness with severe muscle aches and joint pain, fever and sometimes a rash," the Florida Department of Health in Miami-Dade said in a Monday statement. There are typically no respiratory symptoms. People should protect themselves with insect repellent, empty anything that could hold water to stop mosquito breeding and keep them outside by using window screens and air conditioning, the statement said. The Florida Department of Health in Miami-Dade and Miami-Dade County Mosquito Control are coordinating surveillance, prevention and control efforts, the statement said. MORE: The most annoying season has started in Miami — and the mosquitoes are biting So far this year, 21 cases of dengue have been reported in Miami-Dade, according to the federal Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. For more information, visit the department's mosquito prevention webpage or the weekly mosquito-borne illness surveillance reports . Those who are experiencing any symptoms are asked to call the Department of Health in Miami-Dade at 305-324-2400. Most people infected with dengue don't get symptoms, and many recover within a week, according to the Mayo Clinic. But some do get seriously ill and die. People with severe cases of dengue fever can experience severe stomach pain, persistent vomiting, difficulty or rapid breathing, internal bleeding and organ failure, according to the Mayo Clinic. "Symptoms appear 3-14 days after the bite of an infected mosquito and include sudden onset of fever, severe headache, eye pain, muscle and joint pain," and bleeding, according to the Florida Department of Health. Some people may also experience vomiting and diarrhea. The only way to know if you're sick with dengue is to get tested. The good news is that unlike the flu, dengue is not contagious and can only spread to people by bites from infected mosquitoes. The bad news is that there's no specific treatment for dengue fever. If you do get sick with dengue, the CDC recommends resting as much as possible, staying hydrated and take acetaminophen (Tylenol) to help with fever. Avoid taking "ibuprofen (Advil) or aspirin because they may cause bleeding in more severe cases," as the Miami Herald has previously reported. The best way to reduce your risk of dengue infection is to avoid being bit by the pesky bloodsuckers: ▪ Wear EPA-approved long-sleeve shirts if planning to be outside. And wear pants and socks, too. This may sound blasphemous, especially for those who want to get a tan or were planning to have a "hot girl summer." But if you want to reduce a risk of a bite, leave the shorts, tanks and sandals at home. And make sure to drink lots of water and avoid being out in the sun during the hottest time of the day. You don't want to get heat exhaustion or another heat-related illness. ▪ Use EPA-registered mosquito repellents . The repellents should have DEET, picaridin, oil of lemon eucalyptus, or IR-3535. ▪ Clean out rain gutters. You don't want the gutters clogged with branches and other debris that could make it easier for rain to pool. Check containers, pots, buckets, toys, plastic covers and other items in and around your home to make sure there's no standing water too. Remember, standing water is a mosquito's breeding ground. ▪ Change the water in bird baths, fountains, wading pools and potted plant trays at least once a week, according to the EPA. And if you have a swimming pool, make sure to keep the water treated and circulating. ▪ Replace outdoor lights with yellow "bug lights." These lights are not repellents, but they tend to attract fewer mosquitoes , according to the EPA. Peter Lynch, 61, died at HMP Moorland in South Yorkshire on 19 October last year. B routine priorityPublic-health news

VDH confirms first locally-transmitted dengue fever case in Northern Virginia - InsideNoVa.com

News Brief
Virginia health officials have confirmed the first locally-transmitted case of dengue fever in Northern Virginia, marking a significant public health development. According to the Virginia Department of Health (VDH), the case likely resulted from a mosquito biting an infected international traveler and subsequently transmitting the virus to a local resident. This represents the first instance of local dengue transmission in the region. Health authorities are urging residents to take precautions against mosquito bites, though no additional cases or specific risk assessments have been disclosed. The confirmation underscores the importance of mosquito control measures and awareness of mosquito-borne illnesses in the area.
Original source text (English)

州卫生官员确认迈阿密戴德县和棕榈滩县出现两例登革热病例 - wlrn.org

State health officials confirm two dengue cases in Miami-Dade, Palm Beach Counties - wlrn.org
新闻简报佛罗里达州卫生官员已确认迈阿密戴德县和棕榈滩县出现两例本地传播的登革热病例,使今年当地病例总数达到三例。该公告于 2026 年 8 月 3 日发布,此前希尔斯伯勒县 7 月报告了第一例当地病例。截至 7 月 25 日,全州范围内已确认 49 例与旅行相关的登革热病例,还报告了更多基孔肯雅热和西尼罗河病毒病例。登革热是一种由蚊子传播的疾病,会出现类似流感的症状,包括发烧、肌肉酸痛和关节痛,通常在被蚊子叮咬后 14 天内出现。卫生当局敦促保持警惕,因为这些病例与有关可能削减蚊虫控制服务资金的讨论同时发生。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 8 月 3 日;州卫生官员确认活动日期为星期一(2026 年 8 月 3 日)。
地点与对象
佛罗里达州迈阿密-戴德县和棕榈滩县;两县均确诊本地传播病例。
事件
佛罗里达州卫生部确认了两例本地传播的登革热病例,这是佛罗里达州今年第三例本地传播的 登革热 病例。
规模、影响与风险
2026 年总共报告了 3 例当地登革热病例(7 月希尔斯伯勒县 1 例,迈阿密戴德县和棕榈滩 2 例);截至 7 月 25 日,全州确诊 49 例与旅行相关的登革热病例;没有死亡或严重后果的报告。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员正在监测情况;灭蚊工作正在进行中;除病例确认外,没有详细说明的具体应对措施;来源是 WLRN 公共媒体的完整原始报告。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

State health officials confirm two dengue cases in Miami-Dade, Palm Beach Counties - wlrn.org

News Brief
Florida health officials have confirmed two locally-transmitted dengue cases in Miami-Dade and Palm Beach Counties, bringing the total local cases this year to three. The announcement, made on August 3, 2026, follows the first local case reported in Hillsborough County in July. Statewide, 49 travel-related dengue cases have been confirmed as of July 25, with additional cases of Chikungunya and West Nile virus also reported. Dengue, a mosquito-borne illness, presents flu-like symptoms including fever, muscle aches, and joint pain, typically appearing within 14 days of a mosquito bite. Health authorities are urging vigilance as the cases coincide with discussions about potential funding cuts to mosquito control services.
Original source text (English)

Troubleshooting Environment State health officials confirm two dengue cases in Miami-Dade, Palm Beach Counties WLRN Public Media | By Jenny Staletovich Published August 3, 2026 at 2:06 PM EDT Threads Facebook Twitter Pinterest Bluesky LinkedIn Email Gaston De Cardenas / MIAMI HERALD FILE - Fran Middlebrooks, a groundskeeper at Pinecrest Gardens, uses a blower to spray for mosquitoes in August 2016 during an outbreak of Zika in South Florida. The Florida Department of Health announced Monday that it had confirmed a locally-transmitted case of dengue fever in Palm Beach as well as Miami-Dade County. The report marks the third local case for the mosquito-borne disease this year. In July, officials confirmed the first local case in Hillsborough County. Another 49 travel-related cases of dengue have been confirmed across Florida as of July 25. READ MORE: Cutting Florida property taxes could take a bite out of mosquito control The news comes as voters prepare to decide on a statewide property tax amendment that would cut funding to services, including the state’s independent mosquito control districts. According to state health records, a case of West Nile virus confirmed in Alachua County in May is the only other local case of a mosquito-borne illness. As of July 25, 39 travel cases of Chikungunya have also been reported in counties across the state. Those include 19 in Miami-Dade, three in Palm Beach, two in Monroe and one in Broward. Other travel-related cases of dengue include 21 in Miami-Dade, six in Broward and four in Palm Beach. Health officials say dengue can presents as a flu-like illness with muscle aches, join pain, fever and sometimes a rash. Symptoms can manifest within 14 days after being bitten by an infected mosquito. This is a News In Brief report. Visit WLRN News for in-depth reporting from South Florida and Florida news. Environment Threads Facebook Twitter Pinterest Bluesky LinkedIn Email Jenny Staletovich Jenny Staletovich is WLRN's Environment Editor. She has been a journalist working in Florida for nearly 20 years. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

迈阿密戴德州棕榈滩报告当地出现蚊媒登革热病例 - WUSF

Locally acquired cases of mosquito-borne dengue reported in Palm Beach, Miami-Dade - WUSF
新闻简报佛罗里达州卫生官员已确认棕榈滩县和迈阿密戴德县当地出现 登革热 病例,促使加强蚊子 监测 和预防工作。佛罗里达州卫生部正在与当地蚊子控制机构合作,监测和应对这些病例。登革热由埃及伊蚊传播,会引起类似流感的症状,包括发烧和严重的关节疼痛。虽然佛罗里达州今年报告了三例当地病例(包括 7 月份希尔斯伯勒县的一例),但官员强调通过使用驱虫剂和清除积水进行预防。该州继续针对蚊媒疾病实施 监测,包括西尼罗河病毒和基孔肯雅热。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 8 月 3 日下午 5:21(美国东部时间)。事件日期未明确报告,但病例为截至发布时的最新情况。
地点与对象
佛罗里达州棕榈滩县和迈阿密戴德县。县级以外未明确受影响人口。
事件
佛罗里达州卫生官员报告了当地确诊的蚊媒 登革热 病例。该事件已得到证实并正在调查中。
规模、影响与风险
2026 年,佛罗里达州报告了三例当地登革热病例(7 月份希尔斯伯勒县发生一例,棕榈滩和迈阿密戴德县病例数不详)。没有死亡或严重后果的报告。未明确说明风险级别。
应对、状态与不确定性
佛罗里达州卫生部在这两个县的办公室正在与蚊虫控制机构合作进行监测和应对。监测 包括检测、蚊虫控制和公众教育。没有提供有关受影响个人的更多详细信息。来源是完整的原始报告。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Locally acquired cases of mosquito-borne dengue reported in Palm Beach, Miami-Dade - WUSF

News Brief
Florida health officials have confirmed locally acquired cases of dengue fever in Palm Beach and Miami-Dade counties, prompting enhanced mosquito surveillance and prevention efforts. The Florida Department of Health is collaborating with local mosquito control agencies to monitor and respond to these cases. Dengue, spread by Aedes aegypti mosquitoes, causes flu-like symptoms including fever and severe joint pain. While three local cases have been reported in Florida this year (including one in Hillsborough County in July), officials emphasize prevention through repellent use and standing water removal. The state continues surveillance for mosquito-borne illnesses, including West Nile virus and chikungunya.
Original source text (English)

Subscribe Health News Florida Locally acquired cases of mosquito-borne dengue reported in Palm Beach, Miami-Dade WUSF | By Rick Mayer Published August 3, 2026 at 5:21 PM EDT Facebook Twitter LinkedIn Email Joe Mario Pederson / Central Florida Public Media Aedes aegypti mosquitos in a case at the Orange County Mosquito Control headquarters in Orlando. The Florida Department of Health offices in both counties said they are working with mosquito control agencies to monitor and respond to the cases. Florida health officials have confirmed locally acquired cases of dengue fever in Palm Beach and Miami-Dade counties, prompting mosquito surveillance and prevention efforts. The state Department of Health offices in both counties said they are working with their respective mosquito control agencies to monitor and respond to the cases. The offices did not provide additional details about the individuals who contracted the virus. Dengue is spread through the bite of an infected female Aedes aegypti mosquito and is not transmitted person-to-person. The mosquito is known for laying eggs that can be dormant for up to eight months until rains come, leading to a population boom. ALSO READ: Google wants to release 32 million invasive mosquitoes in Florida. Here's why Florida recorded 62 locally acquired dengue cases in 2025, with an unusual concentration in Brevard County, which reported 35 cases — more than half of the state total. Miami-Dade reported 25 locally acquired cases last year. ALSO READ: Cutting Florida property taxes could take a bite out of mosquito control Symptoms typically appear within two weeks of a mosquito bite and can include fever, severe muscle and joint pain, and sometimes a rash. The illness often resembles the flu but usually does not cause respiratory symptoms. Patients typically recover within a couple of weeks. ALSO READ: Less buzzing now, but summer rain will bring a Florida mosquito boom Three local dengue cases have now been reported in the state this year, with one in Hillsborough County in July. There were 49 travel-related cases as of July 25, including 21 in Miami-Dade. Officials are urging residents to prevent mosquito bites by using insect repellent, wearing long sleeves and pants when possible, and removing standing water where mosquitoes can breed. The health department said it would continue statewide surveillance for mosquito-borne illnesses, including West Nile virus, Eastern equine encephalitis, St. Louis encephalitis, malaria, chikungunya and dengue. According to the state arbovirus database, a case of West Nile virus in Alachua County in May is the only other local case of a mosquito-borne illness in 2026. There have been 39 travel cases of Chikungunya this year: Miami-Dade (19), Hillsborough (8), Palm Beach (3), two each in Monroe and Orange, and one each in Alachua, Broward, Hernando, Okaloosa and Osceola. Residents are encouraged to report dead birds to the Florida Fish and Wildlife Conservation Commission. Dead birds can be evidence that a mosquito virus is active nearby. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Virginia Confirms First Locally Acquired Dengue Case - 930 WFMD Free Talk

News Brief
Virginia has confirmed its first locally acquired case of dengue fever, marking a significant public health development. The case, reported by 930 WFMD Free Talk on August 5, 2026, represents the first instance of the virus being acquired within the state rather than through travel. While details about the patient's condition, potential exposures, and public health response measures remain limited in the available information, this confirmation underscores the growing concern of locally transmitted mosquito-borne illnesses in the region. Health officials are likely to monitor the situation closely to assess any potential spread and implement appropriate control measures.
Original source text (English)

武田致力于将登革热 疫苗 纳入印度疫苗接种计划 - 日经亚洲

Takeda aims for dengue vaccine inclusion in India's shots program - Nikkei Asia
新闻简报Japanese pharmaceutical company Takeda is seeking to have its dengue vaccine Qdenga included in India's national immunization program. This follows the recent approval of Qdenga by India's Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation, making it the first vaccine ever approved for dengue prevention in the country. The move comes as India reported 120,000 dengue cases last year. Takeda's senior executive confirmed the company's goal to integrate the vaccine into India's shots program, though no timeline for implementation has been disclosed. The approval marks a significant step in combating dengue, a disease that poses a substantial public health challenge in India.
